863. GEORGE PILFOLD (58) , For the wilful murder of Louisa Pilfold.
MESSRS. POLAND and WILLIAMS Prosecuted; MR. W. SLEIGH Defended.
GEORGE PILFOLD . I used to live at home with my father and mother, and my deaf and dumb brother, at 9, Windmill Street, New Cut—we all occupied one room—I work in the Cattle Market, marking sheep on the back—on Sunday, 3rd August, about 7 am., I went out with a shoeblack box—I returned about 1.30 p.m., which was my dinner-time—my mother was intoxicated, and out at my married sister's, Louisa, so I had no dinner—about 2.30 I went to my sister's to fetch my mother, but she would not come, and I went back home—my brother William came in about 3.30 or 4—my father and I laid down to go to sleep, and in the evening my father woke up and went out, leaving William and me at home—that was about 5.30 or 5.45—I then went to sleep, and when I woke up ray brother had gone out and I was alone—my father and mother came in about 9.30 or 9.45, and my father said, "Are you going out to-night to work, George?"—I said, "Yes, father, I am going out to work"—they were very drunk, but my mother was the most so—I washed myself, and went out to the Crown public-house, where I found my brother William—we had some beer together, and he left me, saying that he was going home—I stopped there till 10.30, and then went to the corner of the coffee-stall at the corner of the Cut—while I was there at 12 o'clock William came to me—he cannot speak, but in consequence of the signs he made I ran home, leaving my coffee and
money—I was changing 6 d. at the time—I ran upstairs and found my mother dead—as I went into the room she was lying on the left-hand side against the wall, as if she had laid herself down there—my father was lying beside her asleep—I felt her, and she was quite cold—I ran down, and told the landlady, and went straight for a policeman, leaving only my father and mother in the room—I found my brother waiting at the corner of Windmill Street as I came back with two policemen, and he and I and the policemen went straight home—we all four went into the room, and my father was lying fast asleep by the side of my mother—one of the policemen went with me to Kennington Lane Station, and I came back with him and the inspector—my father was then sitting on a box, and my mother by his side—my father said, "Who brought these policemen"—I said, "I did, father"—I was going to a cupboard to get a handkerchief, and he said, "Let them things be; they don't belong to you"—the things were a handkerchief and some clean clothes.
Cross-examined. I shall be 19 in December, and my brother William is 24—my father was always kind to my mother and to us—my brother William is very spiteful—I have had to go to the police-station three or four times through his knocking me about—he has stabbed my father—I was present and saw him do it—he was locked up for it, and my father was bail for him to keep the peace—I was at the police-court—I did not go to 2, Windmill Street to get my brother's clothes—that is next door to the Windmill public-house—I was not in the Windmill that night—it is kept by a person named Louisa, not by Cole—I was the first to find out that my brother's clothes were there—he showed them to me after we had been to the police-station—I then went back to get my change and hare my coffee—he had on a butcher's linen smock, which reached "pretty nearly down to his ancles—it was covered with blood—his trousers were saturated with blood, and there was blood inside the flap where the buttons are—these are them—when he showed me the trousers I saw the blood—I had seen the blood on the smock, and saw that it was blood; it was still wet—I had not said anything to him before he showed them to me; he did so of his own accord.
WILLIAM PILFOLD . ( This witness being deaf and dumb, and not understanding the meaning of words, gave his evidence by gestures, which were interpreted by Mr. Whatley, of the Deaf and Dumb Asylum.) On Sunday, 3rd August, I was living with my father and mother at 9, Windmill Street—I was out during that day, and cams home at night; I was there at about 10 o'clock at night—my father and mother were drinking beer; my mother was sitting very close to the fireplace—they began fighting, and then he stabbed her with a small penknife, which he took from his pocket, on the lower part of the stomach—her clothes were over her—I saw him open the knife—when he stabbed her she fell off the chair where she was sitting and fell back on the floor; she did not move at all; she was lying dead; I took her and carried her over and put her on the bed close to the wall; there was no bedstead, the bed was right on the floor—my father told me to do so ( The interpreter here said that the witness meant that his brother George told him to do so)—I lifted her on to the bod before I went for the police—my brother George was there when my father stabbed my mother, and he saw it—when I fetched the police my father and mother were there and the beer was there, and there was nobody else till they came—George was in the room; he did not go with me when I
fetched the police; he remained in the room—when I moved ray mother from the floor there was blood on the floor by the fireplace, and there was blood on my clothes afterwards, but not before I moved her—my father was on the bed when I moved my mother; I saw him lay himself down there about ten minutes after he stabbed my mother—I went downstairs, and George and I told the police, but I told them first—it was about. 10 o'clock when my father stabbed my mother, and I saw the blood on the floor with a candle, and about 11 o'clock I was in a public-house with my, brother; that is just round the corner; I don't know the name; I saw my brother George there, and told him to fetch the police—I brought some beer to my father from the public-house—I can read and write—after I spoke to my brother he and I went upstairs with a policeman to our room—I don't know a coffee-stall near the public-house—I know a beerhouse—I told my brother in our room, to look at my clothes; that was the first time that I had shown them to him—I afterwards washed my trousers at a neighbour's—I don't know whether that is No: 2, Windmill Street; it is in the same street; that was on a Monday, and they lent me an old pair of trousers to wear—my brother George was not in the room when my father stabbed my mother; I first saw him at the coffee-stall; I fetched him; I was drunk.
Cross-examined. I saw my father stab my mother, but not George—I said at the police-court that I changed my trousers, and that my brother George saw me do it—I said first that I changed them in the yard, and afterwards that I changed them at a neighbour's; I left them in a tub of, water there; I did not fetch them—my brother found them there, and gave them to the police—I did not before the police were brought in go into the yard and empty some dirty water, throw it into the yard, take some clean water, and put it into my mother's room—I did go into the yard; there is a water tap there; that is where we go for clean water when we want it—I know some people named Driver; I passed Martha Driver and went into the yard, and threw some dirty water away and took some clean water upstairs—I did not take any water upstairs—this knife belongs to my father and this other to my mother; I saw this one on that night ( A brown one)—my mother was sitting quietly on a chair when she was stabbed, but my father was not—she had all her clothes on except her bonnet—after the stab she fell back; the stab was like this ( A thrust); the knife passed right through her clothes—I have been locked up for stabbing my father in the street; he was taken to the hospital, and very nearly died—I have never struck my mother; I liked her; she was good; she was quiet; we never quarrelled; she often got drunk—I have struck my father on other occasions—I dislike him.
Re-examined. I was in custody four days when I was charged with stabbing my father.
LOUISA MARKS . I am the wife of Samuel Marks, a porter, of 30, King Street, New Cut—the prisoner is my father, and the deceased was my mother—on Sunday, 3rd August, about 8 a.m., my mother came to my house; I let her in, and gave her 4 d. I owed her—she remained with me all day, and at 9 we both went out with ray husband, my father, a friend, and two children—my father came at 7.35 to see if my mother was coming home—she said she was coming in a minute, and he went out—he wanted to pay for the victuals she had had—I offered him a glass of ale, and he
refused it—he was very tipsy—my mother was as sober as I am at this moment—we all went down King Street and up William Street to the Lord Hill public-house, where we had three quarterns of gin, and my father paid for a pot of ale—he took some of the gin every time—and he took a quartern of gin in my room before we went out, and he had some ale in the public-house—my father and mother took more than I did—we then went to another public-house, where we all partook of two quarterns of gin and a pot of ale—I bade them good night, and we made up our ninds to go to Hampstead on the Monday, and father said, "Have you got money enough, girl I if you have not, I have"—my mother and all were to go—I left them outside the public-house, and they left as if to go home; my mother was then getting the worse for liquor—at 12 that night, in consequence of what two women told me, I went round to my mother's house, which was three minutes' walk, went up to her room, and saw her lying on the bed dead—my father was sitting on a chair, my deaf and dumb brother was silting by the window, and two policemen were in the room—I said, "Oh, father, father, mother is killed; who has done it I—"he said, "What is the matter, gal?" and I said, in an excited way, "It is no more than I expected '—I was then sent out of the room by a gentleman, a doctor, I believe—my father said, "It's a good job she is dead"—I said, "You. don't mean it, father"—he said nothing—a woman was afterwards called for, and I rushed up and took her clothes off, and out of her pocket fell this little white handled knife, which I had used to scrape some potatoes that morning, and three pawn tickets.
Cross-examined. Before my father said it's a good job she is dead, he said, "What is the matter I have not done anything"—he was sitting in a chair behind the cupboard door, on the right hand side of the room, opposite the window—he said he was asleep in bed with mother when they brought the police in—the knife fell out of my mother's pocket as I cut her clothes down the front and lifted her—they only occupied one room—there were two wooden tubs there, they would hold water—I know who lives at 2, Windmill Street, but I don't know their names—it is a fact that my brother is a spiteful, ill-tempered fellow—he has attacked me twice, and my mother also with his fist, on a Sunday afternoon; that was done in a savage, violent way—he drinks very much—as far as my judgment goes, though he cannot speak, he is not wanting in cleverness and acuteness; he is very artful—on the day we were talking of going to Hampstead, my father was kind to my mother; they were going to have a nice family party, and tbey were on good terms together—No words passed between them up to the time—I left them, and they seemed happy—they left me at 9.15, and I was fetched at 11.45—my deaf and dumb brother disliked my father very much.
Re-examined. My deaf and dumb brother struck me with his fist and knocked me down in the yard—he came to my place when my husband was out of work, and we could not give him a good Sunday's dinner, because mother had been living with us, and he struck me a blow in the face with his fist, and he attacked my mother that very Sunday because she took my part—he is hot-tempered and very spiteful when he drinks—he was fond of his mother occasionally, but she was in the habit of selling her things for drink, and then he got excited.
WILLIAM DRIVER . I live at 9, Windmill Street, New Cut, and am the landlord of the house—the prisoner and his wife and two sons lived
with me about five months; I can't be certain to a week—I have seen the prisoner and his wife fresh, but not often—on Sunday, August 3rd, I went to bed between 8 and 9 o'clock—my room is upstairs, on the same floor as theirs—there is no other room on that floor—they were out when I went to bed—I afterwards heard them go upstairs, I think it was between 9 and 10 o'clock, but I was in the dark, and there was no clock—I heard the prisoner say to his son, "Are you going out to-nigbt, George?"—George replied, "Yes, father, I am going out"—I then heard George or some one go downstairs, and after that there was a little quarrel, and the prisoner said to his wife, "You b——old cow go and fetch me some beer and some cheese"—he also called her a b——old whore—I heard her go downstairs; she returned in a few minutes, and then there was a quarrel, and a repetition of the language I had heard before—I did not hear her say anything—I heard a scuffle, and something like a candlestick falling down, and then a thumping noise and a groan—I did not get up—my wife was not there—I went to sleep, and heard no more till the deaf and dumb boy came upstairs: that woke me up—I did not see him, but I knew him by the noise, because he makes a great deal more than anybody else, as he cannot hear—I had not seen him that Sunday.
Cross-examined. The bed is on the floor, there is no bedstead—the noise I heard may have been caused by the prisoner throwing himself down on the bed to go to sleep.
MARTHA DRIVER . I am the wife of the last witness—the prisoner and his wife and two sons occupied two rooms on our first floor—I was standing at the gate on Sunday night, 3rd August, and saw the prisoner and his wife come in—as far as I know it had just turned 9.30—they were both the worse for drink, the prisoner appeared the worst—a quarter of an hour after I heard George come downstairs and go out into the street; the deceased then came down and spoke to my daughter, she seemed to walk straight enough—in consequence of what she said to my daughter she went and fetched some beer for her, and the deceased went across the road and bought some cheese—after my daughter came hack with the beer, I went across the road and bought a candle for the deceased, which I gave her with a half-penny change; she waited at the door for it, she said the half-penny would get her a half-pint to-morrow—she went into her room which is just over mine—I went to my room and heard the prisoner swear at her and use very bad words, which I cannot make use of they were so dreadful, but leaving them out he said to his wife, "l Look here, I will put your b——light out to-night"—I did not see George come in—I was in bed when he knocked at our door; I heard the deaf and dumb boy come in at a quarter to 11, that was half an hour after I heard the words about putting the light out; 1 heard someone I believe to be him go upstairs, and about five minutes after the deaf and dumb boy came in—he came down with some water and put it on the stairs; it was in a little long tub—I heard him bump the tub down on the floor, and he spilt the water on the stairs—he came down again in five minutes and made signs to me which I could not understand—he went upstairs again and came running down directly and went up the street to fetch his brother—he was gone ten minutes; I did not see them return, but I heard them—they went up to their room, and not more than five minutes after they both came down again, and George knocked at our door—I opened it and he said "My mother is dead;" they went out again and came back with the police.
Cross-examuied. I heard a noise as if some one was tapping on the hearth; I did not say that it was as if someone was walking across the floor; I heard no scuffle or screaming—it was half an hour after the last noise I heard, that the deaf and dumb boy went up to the room—he was under the influence of liquor when I saw him come in.
LYDIA DRIVER . I am single, and live at 9, Windmill Street—on Sunday, 3rd August, at 10.30, I was standing with my mother and sister in the street; the deceased came down and I went and got some beer for her, I gave it to her and she went upstairs with it—she had been drinking, but she could walk straight—soon after she went up I was standing under the window of her room, which is the first-floor front and about 8 feet above my head, and heard the prisoner say to her, I will put your light out before the niglit is over—I went to get my supper and heard no more, nor did I hear any one go upstairs, but I saw the deaf and dumb boy come in at a quarter to 11; I did not see George that night.
ELIZA DRIVER . I am single, and live at 9, Windmill Street—on Sunday night, 3rd August, I was standing outside the door with my mother and bister and heard the prisoner and his wife quarrelling upstairs; he said, "I will put your b——light out before the night is over," after that I heard a thumping noise—while I was standing outside the deaf and dumb boy came down and made some signs to me which I could not understand—he went upstairs again, stayed three minutes, and came down and went up the street with his brother George, who said something to my mother; they bath went away and returned with the police.
Cross-examined. They often used to quarrel—the woman used to drink, but I never heard her use bad language—I had never heard the prisoner say before "I will put your light out".
JAMES WALSH ( L 209). On 3rd August, after midnight, George Pilfold and his dumb brother came to me together—George spoke to me and I went with them to 9, Windmill Street, to a room on the first floor, where I found the prisoner partly undressed and apparently asleep; a woman was lying on her back on the part of the bed next to the wall, her clothes were wised up to her knees—I saw she was dead—I saw some blood on the hearth, and sent 40 l., who was with me, for an inspector and a doctor—the prisoner lay still asleep, and in a short time another constable came, bringing the dumb son, who had left the room, and who woke his father up and pointed to the dead body of the woman—the prisoner said "What is the use of taking notice of that b——drunken cow, she has been drunk like that for the last month, and a b——good job if she is dead who the b—hell fetched you two b——great fellows to me in my own room 1 I suppose it was that lazy young swine, George; how is it that he has not gone to work? if you don't let me out I will jump through the window"—he went towards the window and partially raised it up, but was prevented by another constable—the inspector then came, and Mr. Wilson, the doctor—I found this pearl-handled knife under the dead woman's body when Louisa Marks was taking off the clothes for the doctor to examine—I also found some halfpence and a farthing on the bed and took them—nobody had interfered with the bed till the inspector came—the woman was on the hed, as if she had been put there—she was on her back, and her legs were stretched out straight.
Cross-examined. She was lying next to the wall, by her husband, her
head at the top and her feet at the bottom—I say that she looked as if she had been put there, from the position of her clothes; they were in such a position that if a person had passed his hand up her legs they would hare been pushed up for that purpose.
HENRY WHITNEY . I was a police-inspector at this time, and on duty at Kennington Road Station, when George Pilfold and Constable 40 l. came to me about 12.20, and from what they said I went to 9, Windmill Street, and saw the deceased's body lying on the bed next to the wall—the prisoner and his dumb son were there, and Walsh—on the right side of the hearth I found a patch of blood about 12 inches by 8, and a great quantity of blood about the room and about the bedding—the woman's clothes were quite saturated—I sent for a doctor—I asked the prisoner his name—he said "George Pilfold," and that he was a tinplate worker—I asked him if the deceased was his wife—he said "She is not a b——wife to me, a b——good job if she was dead"—I said "I am afraid she is dead"—the doctor then came—I asked the prisoner if he had a knife—he handed me a small white-handled knife, and said "This is what I use as a toothpick"—he proceeded to take the things out of his pocket, and I said "I will search you myself"—in the inside breast-pocket of his coat I found this brown-handled knife—I could trace no blood on either of the knives—I saw blood on the dumb boy's clothes, and asked the prisoner how his son came by it—he said "He got that by lying down there," pointing to the blood on the hearth—the dumb boy made some signs, and another brother who was in the room, not George, motioned to him to be quiet—I know that it meant that because he ceased directly—the sergeant was acquainted with the sign language a little, and he commenced to communicate with the dumb boy, and the same son said "He won't tell him anything, let him stop till tomorrow and go to the gentleman at the schools. ( The interpreter)—I said that I should take the prisoner in custody on suspicion of murdering his wife—he said "I will go with you"—I examined him, and found no blood on him, and I handed him over to Sergeant Hawkins—I measured the room—it measures 11 feet by 10—I made a survey, and there was no sign of any struggle taking place—the bed is a piece of sacking with some flock—I also looked at the chairs, but found no blood on them.
Cross-examined. There was nothing inconsistent with them both having laid down and gone to sleep that night—the prisoner had not his coat and waistcoat off when I went in—it was not necessary for me to search him—he would have given me everything willingly—he did not make a show of resistance, or give me any trouble—he was drunk, and I do not think he knew what he was about.
JAMES WALSH ( Re-examined.) I saw the prisoner woke up by his son—he was lying on the bed with his coat and waistcoat off—they were on a chair.
JAMES HAWKINS ( Police Sergeant). On the morning of 4th August the inspector gave the prisoner into my charge—I took him to the station, and he made a statement on the road which I put down directly I got to the station—he said, "When you have got hold of a bad partner you never know what is going to happen—I have had 38 years of it—she has not been in doors one night this week—I was fast asleep when you chaps came up"—I said, "Do you mean you were asleep in that room?"—he said, "Yes, you call it a mechanic's room; there is nothing much there; whatever
I get there always goes to his legs"—he volunteered that statement—I never spoke to him except to hurry him on when he did not look sharp—he appeared very muddled and unsteady, as if he was recovering from drink; but he could walk very well.
ELIZABETH BODEMAN . I live at 2, Windmill Street,. New Cut—on Tuesday, 5th August, about 2 o'clock in the day, the deaf and dumb boy was by my door with a considerable number of people—I had seen him once or twice before—my brother made some signs to him to get him out of the crowd, instead of which he thought my brother invited him in, and he came in—we were at dinner with Mary Ann Davis—I made motions to him, trying to convey that we would give him something to eat; and he made motions with his mouth, as if to convey that he would have something to eat—I cut him some bread and meat and handed him a knife—he put the knife on one side and shuddered at it—he would not look at it—he ate the bread and meat and went away—about 5 p.m. he came in again when my brother and I and Mrs. Davis were at tea—I offered him, by signs, a cup of tea—he pointed to his mouth again, and I made him two cups and gave him some bread and butter—he pointed to some blood on his trousers; I motioned to ask him how he came by it—he lifted something up from the fire-place and made a motion as if he was lifting something into the bed or sofa. ( MR. SLEIGH objected to this evidence as it teas a conversation, though by dumb show, in the prisoner's absence, equivalent to words; and being so, he was unable to check it. MR. WILLIAMS contended that the evidence was admissible, the signs being acts done. MR. JUSTICE LOPES considersd that the signs were something more than acts done, and equivalent to an ordinary conversation, and therefore excluded the evidence.) In consequence of what passed my brother gave the deaf and dumb boy an old pair of trousers, and he took off his own trousers, put them into a tub and put a little cold water on them—he wrote something on a piece of paper—he came again on Wednesday and Thursday, and seemed in a way because I had put his trousers in water—the detective subsequently took them away—they had remained up to that time in the water—they had not been washed at all, only put in the water and run out again.
Cross-examined. I know the eldest brother—he was at our house before the police came—he asked me if I had got any clothes of his brother's there, but his brother had been before that—a policeman who is here took the clothes away on the same day that the elder brother came, but I can't say how long after—the deaf and dumb boy was in a way because I soaked his trousers—this young man came with detective Whitney.
MARY ANN DAVIS . I was present on the Tuesday when the dumb boy came to Mrs. Bodeman's house.
HENRY WILSON , M.R.C.S. I live at 33, Great Charlotte Street—I was called on the night of 3rd August and found the body of the deceased lying on a bed on the floor; the prisoner was there and Inspector Whitney and one or two constables—I first examined the body in the position in which it was lying and discovered a large quantity of blood on the clothes which were covered with liquid motion which had escaped with the blood—I could not see externally where the blood proceeded from on account of the dirt which was upon the body, and requested that a woman should be sent for to wipe away some of the dirt and help to remove the clothes—I then saw blood flowing from the vagina, but there
was no wound or abrasion outside the vagina. ( A diagram was here put in showing the situation of the wound, and this part of the evidence was unfit for publication.) The inspector showed me the small blade of a knife which fitted the wound exactly, but it might have been done by any penknife—I made a post-mortem examination next day with Dr. Cyril Jones—the wound could have been inflicted while she was lying on the bed, but I do not think it could have been done if she was sitting flat on a chair—her clothes were very scanty—her age was 59; the brain was healthy, but almost bloodless, and the body was very empty of blood—there was a slight bruise on the right temple, and a deal of bruising under the scalp and extravasated blood—if she was sitting on a low chair and in front of the edge it might have been inflicted sitting, but I did not notice the height of the chair—I noticed a patch of blood on the hearth, it appeared to be bright arterial blood, which would be the first to flow from such a wound—my opinion is that it was inflicted near the hearth; it was the only clotted blood, the ordinary blood was soaked into the bedclothes—when the wound was first inflicted the blood would flow more freely, and being in larger quantity it would not be absorbed by the clothes—there would probably not be time for blood to be on the knife—that is the result of an experiment I made that night—my idea is that the knife was inserted under the clothes—the whole-handled knife which fell from the woman's body would inflict the wound; there would be very little difference—the blood would certainly spurt from the arteries very quickly.
Cross-examined. If she had been drinking heavily she might sleep very heavily, and the insertion of the knife without violence might not wake her, but it might have been done by a stab.**********
Re-examined. I think the wound might have been done in any position if there had been a struggle—supposing she was sitting on the edge of the chair it could be done in that position.
By the COURT. It is very unlikely that it was done by a stab, but if it was she must have been sitting on the edge of the chair—the prisoner was in a maudlin state when I saw him; he was sitting on a chair for the first 20 minutes, but he walked out of the room pretty well—his hand was steady enough to take the knife out of his pocket—I do not think it would require any remarkable steadiness of hand to inflict such a wound.
CYRIL LLOYD JONES , M. D., M. R. C. S. I live in Blackfriars road—I assisted Mr. Wilson at the post-mortem examination—I do not know that I differ from him in any material particular, but I do not think it possible to say whether it was done in the way he suggests or by a sudden push and cut up—I do not think it likely that it was done by stabbing through the clothes. ( The remainder of this witness's evidence was unfit for publication.)
WILLIAM PILFOLD ( Re-examined). There was blood on the inside of the flap of my trousers—they were buttoned up at the time I removed my mother to the bed—it got there by carrying her—it was outside.
GEORGE PILFOLD ( Re-examined). When my brother showed me his trousers there was blood inside and outside them—I won't be sure whether it had soaked through, but it had run in, and there was blood on his waistcoat, and his shirt and smock were saturated; the shirt had more than the trousers—when I came in I found my mother laid on the bed against the
wall and my father outside; nobody could have got to the side where she was without lifting her over my father.
GUILTY of manslaughter. — Twenty Years' Penal Servitude.
Before Mr. Justice Manisty.
[trialAccount t18790915-864]
864. CHARLOTTE CUTLER (23) was indicted for the wilful murder of Frederick Ward.
MESSRS. POLAND and MONTAGU WILLIAMS Prosecuted; MESSRS. WARNER SLEIGH and AUSTIN METCALFE Defended.
LUCT WHITBREAD . I am head nurse at the infirmary at Kingston Union—I know the prisoner by the name of Alice Ward; she was admitted into the infirmary by that name on 3rd June—she was in labour when she came and on the 4th I delivered her of a male child; it was born with a double caul over its face—on 2nd July she was discharged with her baby—during the time she was in the workhouse she treated the baby kindly; it was a very nice child—she nursed and suckled it and dressed it after the fortnight, and behaved to it as a good mother—when she left with it on 2nd July it was to all appearance in a healthy state—she told me she was going to Weybridge to her mother's—the child was vaccinated before it left the Union; it was done twice; the first time the arm did not take, and she had to stay another week on account of the baby's arm—when it left it had on a little nightgown, a petticoat, a flannel belly-band, and a diaper—the band was hemmed instead of herring-boned; I called her attention to that—I have since seen a belly-band and diaper produced by the police; they were the same that the child had on when it left the workhouse—I have not seen the nightgown or petticoat; there was what is called a back flannel all to pieces, I could not identify that—this ( produced) is the diaper; it is an old calico one with a hole in the corner—I identify that and this belly-band by being hemmed instead of herring-boned; I never saw one hemmed before.
Cross-examined. The prisoner paid every attention to the child while she was at the Union—the fact of the diaper being made of calico is not extraordinary—I can't say that the hole in the corner is uncommon, it may not be—it is the diaper the child had on—I have been four years a nurse at Isleworth—I never saw a diaper like this—I have certainly seen diapers with holes where the safety pin goes; it is not usual to pin them in the corner like this—I was examined twice before the Magistrate; I don't recollect saying that I only saw the articles once—I don't think I did—my deposition was read over, and I signed it—I am quite sure the child was a healthy child—it did not refuse the breast—she was not nursing other children to my knowledge—I never saw her do it—I could not always be in one ward; there are a hundred patients to see to—I am the only person here from the infirmary—she might have nursed other children without my knowing it.
MARY EDWARDS . I keep the White Horse Inn at Norbiton; that is about 10 minutes' walk from the Kingston Union Infirmary—on a Wednesday morning in the beginning of July, between 10 and 11 o'clock, the prisoner came into the house with a baby—she had a glass of beer—my niece called my attention to the child, because it was a fine baby, and I went into the tap-room where she was sitting, and looked at it; it was a very fine baby—she said she had come from the infirmary, and she had to wait till 4 o'clock to
register the baby—she said it was born with a caul over its head—she left and afterwards returned, and said she had been to register the baby and back to the infirmary to take the register—she said the nurse would not allow her to have the caul, and I told her to ask Mr. Clark at the union—she left just before 4 o'clock, and said she was going to Weybridge, to her mother's—she did not mention her name or say anything more about herself—I gave her some tea and something to eat; I did not give her any money—I saw no more of her—it was a very nice baby, and appeared to be all right.
Cross-examined. She seemed very fond of it—I know nothing of the bodies of several children being found in the neighbourhood lately—nothing was said to me about her nursing other children in the workhouse.
JOHN HARRIS . I live at No. 2, Richmond Villas, Kingston-on-Thames—I am registrar of births and deaths for the Kingston sub-district—on 2nd July I registered the birth of a male child, by a person who gave the name of Alice Ward—the name was put down as "Frederick," the date of the birth 4th June, and the address "Oatlands Park, "Weybridge"—my place is about 100 yards from the White Horse.
JAMES LANGRIDGB . I am a pork butcher, of 23, Garden Row, Mortlake—on Friday afternoon, 18th July, about 2.30,1 was walking along the Lower Mortlake Road, and saw the body of a child in a ditch; I was on the pathway, and I crossed the road to look at some mowers in a field, and on looking in the ditch saw the body of the child; there was water in the ditch, and the back part of the head was partly out of the water—I called the attention of the mowers to the child, and then went and fetched a policeman—I saw him take the child out of the ditch—there were some articles of clothing on it—I saw them taken off afterwards; there was a flannel band and a diaper; that was all I saw.
JAMES WARNE ( Policeman V 261). On Friday, 18th July, in consequence of information from Mr. Langridge, I went with him to the Lower Mortlake Road, and there saw the dead body of a male child in a ditch—I conveyed it to the mortuary at Mortlake, and a doctor was afterwards called and the child was undressed—this belly-band and diaper are what it was wealing—there were no other articles upon the child.
Cross-examined. I did not search the house of the prisoner's mother.
SAMUEL SAMPSON ( Police Sergeant, Scotland Yard). On 19th July I received instructions to make inquiries about this child, and on the evening of the 24th I saw the prisoner at her father's house, at West End, Mortlake—that is between 300 and 400 yards from the ditch where the child was found—I told her I was a detective sergeant, and from information I had received I believed she had recently been confined of a child—she said she had—I asked her where the child was—she said "My child is all right"—I said "But where is your child?"—she again said "My child is all right"—I asked her the third time, and she repeated again "My child is all right and at Weybridge"—I said "Who has charge of your child at Weybridge?" she made no answer—I said "You refuse to give me any further account of your child any more than it is all right at Weybridge?" she said "Yes"—I asked where she was confined; she said at the Kingston Union—I asked in what name; she said "Alice Ward"—I then left to make further inquiries—I did not search the house—I went to Weybridge and endeavoured to trace the child, but failed to do so—on 4th August I went to the house again with Inspector Shaw and saw the prisoner at the door
—he told her who we were and that we had come to make inquiries respecting her child, and he said "Where is your child?"—she then asked us to come inside, and she led us through the passage into the kitchen—as loon as we got into the kitchen a man came in who said he was a solicitor, and he told the prisoner not to answer any questions or say anything—I requested him to go away but he would not, and as he annoyed us I went out to see if I could find an officer in uniform, so I did not hear anything farther—I left Inspector Shaw there—afterwards at the railway station at Mortlake the prisoner said "Is there a man named Pink in trouble?"—I said "No"—she said "He ought to be; he is the father of my child; I should have summoned him only I hare sworn one child already, and they asked so many funny questions I did not like to do so"—her mother lived at this house as well as her father—I know that they have lived there over 12 months—no articles of clothing were found there—the frock and petticoat said to have left the workhouse have never been traced.
Cross-examined. I did not hear Inspector Shaw caution her that what she said would be used in evidence against her—I believe there have been two bodies of children found besides this one; not in the exact neighbourhood, some distance off, one at Barnes and one at Kew; that would be a distance of about a mile or a mile and a half—I know the ditch in question—I believe it is not a tidal ditch; the water runs one way, but not very strongly.
Re-examined. It is a continual steady flow, not sufficient to move anything the size of this child—I cannot tell where the other bodies were found—I had nothing to do with those cases.
EDWARD SHAW ( Police Inspector). On 4th August, between 1 and 2 in the afternoon, I went with Sampson to the residence of the prisoner's father at Mortlake—I knocked at the door, the prisoner answered it—I asked her her name—she said, "Charlotte Cutler"—I told her who I was—I was in plain clothes—I said I wished to speak to her—she said, "Come outside"—I said, "No, I would rather see you iuside the house—she took me into the back part of the house; there was a young woman there—I then asked the prisoner if she could tell me where her child was that she had been confined of—she said, "My child is all right"—I said, "Where?"—at this time I was interrupted by a gentleman who came into the wash-house and said he was a solicitor—I told him not to interrupt me in the execution of my duty—he got out a piece of paper and a pencil, and told the prisoner not to say anything—I then spoke to her again about the child—she said, "If my sister goes out I will tell you"—the sister went out and the gentleman also—the prisoner then said, "I will tell you now all about it; I left Kingston Union on the 2nd or 3rd of last month, I went into a public-house and had some refreshment; I took the train to Kingston, at St. Margaret's I got out and I walked to Mortlake, and while I was on the bridge I met a woman with a child, and I gave her a shilling to take the child away"—she said she did not want to disgrace her family by taking the child home—she did not at that time say where the woman was to take the child—I told her I should take her into custody on suspicion of having murdered her child—I then conveyed her to the railway station at Mortlake, and while standing on the platform she said, "There has been three or four children found lately, has there not?"—I said, "Yes"—I then conveyed her to Richmond police-station—I afterwards heard her
make a further statement, which Inspector Fairy took down in writing the same day.
Cross-examined. The gentleman I speak of is sitting in Court, I did not know him—he did not ask what I was doing there—I was in plair clothing—I told him three or four times that I was an officer of police, but I could not convince him—he asked to see my card; I showed it to tin—he told the prisoner not to answer any questions—I did not caution her in any way, or tell her that what she said might be used in evidence against her.
Re-examined. I was making inquiries.
DAVID FAIRY ( Police Inspector V). On 4th August the prisoner was brought to the Richmond station by Inspector Shaw on suspicion of murder ing her child—I said, "You are not called upon to say anything before 1 read the charge, but should you say anything, what you say will be taken down in writing, and used as evidence against you"—she burst into tears, and then made this statement, which I wrote down—( Read:) "I only wish to say that I did not murder my child, and I was in hopes he was alive; I gave him to a person on the bridge near the gas factory, and I asked her if she would take it to the Richmond Union for me, and I gave her a shilling, that was all the money I was possessed of, and she promised me faithfully she would take it the next morning."
BENJAMIN BILLING . I am porter at the Richmond Union "Workhouse—I keep the book of children admitted to the workhouse—there was no child left at the workhouse without the mother about this time in July—one child was admitted in July; the mother is now in the Asylum and the father took the child away.
ROBERT COCHRANE MULLINS . I am a registered medical practitioner—on 18th July last I was living at Mortlake, and was assistant to Dr. Marshall there—the constable Warne came to me, and in consequence of what he said I went to the mortuary at Mortlake, and there saw the dead body of a male child—I could not recognise the features; the body was in a state of putridity—I could not form any positive opinion as to how long it had been dead; I should say probably about fourteen days; it might possibly have been more; I don't think lesss; it might be—the atmosphere might have a certain degree of influence—there were no marks that I could trace; from the appearance of the body I should not be able to state whether there were any marks of violenoe; I found none—it was a well-developed child—I could not form any opinion as to the cause of death; it was quite impossible to do so; from the state of the body I could form no judgment whether it had died from natural causes or any other—it was difficult to give an opinion as to its age; I did form an opinion that it was between 3 and 6 months old, probably 4; that was not a positive opinion—I came to that conclusion from the structural development—it was impossible to give an opinion as to whether it might only have been 2 or 3 weeks old; one child at 3 or four weeks old may be quite as well developed and as large as one of 2 or 3 months; we meet with such cases every day; it is quite possible that this child may have been born at the beginning of June; my impression was the contrary; it is not probable, it is highly improbable—I could not trace any marks of vaccination; it was impossible to do so, it was a putrid body.
Cross-examined. The average length of a child a month old is somewhat
about 18 inches—the length of this child was 23 1/2; that would he an exceptional length for a child a month old; we hear of monstrosities—decomposition frequently causes a body to contract—this body was contracted—my opinion was that this child was probably 4 and possibly 6 months old; at the same time, from the state of the body, I should not give a definite opinion regarding it—I fix the minimum at 3 and the maximum at 6—my judgment is founded partially on the length of the child and the structural development of the limbs—in the first few months the signs of structural development are pretty well denoted—it is very easy for a medical man to judge of the age of a child by the structural development.
Re-examined. I have been in practice nearly seven years—my knowledge of children arises from what I have seen in practice and from my general knowledge in studying medicine.
LUCY WHITEBREAD ( Re-examined). I have been nurse at the Kingston Union Infirmary since 17th February—I have had sixteen children born there—at the time this inquiry was going on I had a child there a month old.
MR. POLAND proposed to ask the length of that child. MR. SLEIGH objected, and MR. JUSTICE MANISTY expressing a doubt of its admissibility it teas not pressed. MR. SLEIGH then submitted that there was realty no case to go to the Jury, the identification of the body being almost disproved by ike evidence of the surgeon. MR. POLAND relied Upon the contradictory state, mehts of the prisoner and the identification of the two articles of clothing found upon the body of the child. MR. JUSTICE MANISTY considered that there was some evidence upon which to take the opinion of the Jury.
Witnesses for the Defence.
MARY CUTLER . I am the prisoner's mother—I Sent to her in the Work-house a set of clothes for the child of which she was confined; a frock, long petticoat, two woollen shawls, a belly band, a shirt and flannel—I knew that she was, in consequence of her poverty, going to be confined in the union—the belly band I sent her was not hemmed or herring-boned—I never used such a thing—the one I sent was exactly similar to this clean one ( producing it)—I had many of them in use—I am prepared to swear that they were neither hemmed or herring-boned.
Cross-examined. I did not go to the workhouse to see the child—I have never seen it—I did not visit my daughter there—she came back to me on Wednesday evening, 2nd July, between 9 and 10—she had no child with her or any child's clothing—she said she had given it to a strange woman on the bridge by the gas factory to take to the Richmond Union; and that she gave the person a shilling—she had money when she left home—she has been to Weybridge; not since the child was born—she has never been away from home.
Re-examined. I know a man named Pink—I believe he lives in Mortlake—my daughter is a kind, good-dispositioned girl—I have got her other child at home, and have always taken care of it.
JOSEPH ROGERS , M.D., and M.R.C.S. I have been over 30 years in practice, and 23 years surgeon to the Westminster and Strand Union—I have had a large number of children under my notice at the butts and subsequently—I cannot call to mind that I have ever seen a case of a child a month old 23 1/2 inches in length—I do not say it is impossible—
decomposition so advanced, as this seems to have been, would cause diminution in the bulk—at an" earlier period it would probably cause it to be a little longer; but, as it advances, the skin breaks, and from the air that is generated in the body it gets shorter—in the state of decomposition in which this body was I think it would get shorter—I think it is open to the belief that this child was between three and six months old; it might have been three or four months old—I should question its being only a month old—I would not swear positively, but I should question it.
Cross-examined. I did not see the child—I merely infer what I have said from the account of the decomposition.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18790915-865]
865. WILLIAM NORMAN (26) , Feloniously killing and slaying Sarah Norman.
MR. POLAND Prosecuted; MR. VALENTINE BBOWN Defended.
WILLIAM THOMAS PAYNE . I am a fitter, and live at 44, Vauxhall Walk—the deceased, Sarah Norman, was my sister—she was 25 years of age, and was married to the prisoner, who is a coal porter at a gas factory—they were living at 3, Curry Street, Nine Elms, Battersea—they had four children—the youngest was nearly three months old at the time of the accident—I saw my sister dead on the Tuesday morning at St. Thomas' Hospital; after that I saw the prisoner; I said, "You have done a fine thing, young fellow, have you not?"—he said, "Yes, it was entirely her own fault; she aggravated me to do it"—she was a sober woman as far as I knew.
Cross-examined. The last time I saw her was two months before.
MARY ANN KILBY . I am the wife of James Kilby, a dustman, and live at 12, Ponton Street, Battersea—I knew the prisoner and his wife—on Monday evening, 25th August, I saw the deceased at her own door; she spoke to me, and went and laid her baby on the bed asleep, and I went for a walk with her round the houses; we met some friends, and had some drink—we remained out till 10 o'clock—afterwards, about 12 o'clock or half past, she was sitting on the kerb in Curry Street, near her own house; I was with her—we were sober—the prisoner came out of his house; he was dressed, and had the baby underneath his coat—he had his boots on—she said to me "Here is my baby"—he said "Sarah, here is the baby"—she said "Yes, you b——thing, keep it"—he said "So I can"—he put the child into her arms—she took it, and said "I am glad I have got my baby"—he was walking away indoors, and she halloaed after him "Go out you b——half-starved thing, you bastard," and used very foul language to him—in about 20 minutes he came out again to her—she was still sitting on the kerb—she called him names, and said "Look at your b——old wh—of a mother"—with that he walked up, buttoned his coat, and said "Let my mother rest, she is nothing to you"—she said "Yes, the b——old wh—"—he then gave her a kick somewhere about the side; he had his boots on—she fell back, and said "Oh, Bill! oh, Bill! you have done for me"—she had her baby in her arms at the time—he said nothing, but walked away—after she had laid a little while she said "Where is Bill?"—he then came to her, and cried, and took her by the head behind with a pillow, and said "Sarah, Sarah, my girl, speak to me"—she made, no reply—I saw blood underneath where she was lying—a constable came up, and a doctor was
sent for, and she was taken to the hospital in a cab—I could not tell whether the prisoner was drunk or sober.
Cross-examined. He was very fond of his wife, and a very good husband, and very patient—he came into the street twice to try to induce her to come indoors—she would not go, and he could not move her in—I did not see him come out undressed—he is a labourer—I was not drunk, nor was she—we had been two hours in a public-house with one pot of beer.
TIMOTHY DALY . I am a labourer, and live at 4, Currie Street, Nine Elms—I knew the prisoner and his wife—on Monday night I was going home, and saw Mrs. Kilby and Mrs. Norman sitting on the kerb—Mrs. Norman bad the baby—she spoke to me; I could not tell whether she was sober or not—I sat down by them—shortly afterwards the prisoner came out of his house in his night-shirt without his trousers or boots—he asked his wife whether she was coming indoors—she said "No, go away you b——thing"—she used some very bad language to him—he said nothing, but walked upstairs—in about 10 minutes he came out again dressed, and he had his boots on, and I saw him come up and give his wife a kick somewhere about the groin—I could hardly tell what sort of a kick it was—she fell back, and did not speak—afterwards she said "Bill, Bill, you have done for me"—he made no reply—I don't know what became of him—a constable came up shortly afterwards—I saw blood where the woman was lying—a doctor was sent for—I afterwards heard her say "Bill, Bill, kiss me," or "speak to me"—he had come up from round the corner—he did kiss her—she was then taken to the hospital.
Cross-examined. I did not see any one making a mockery of the prisoner when he came out in his night shirt—she used bad language to him before he gave her the kick.
JOHN GARLAND ( Policeman W 383). I was on duty in Currie Street, shortly before 2 o'clock—I saw the deceased lying on the pavement, as if she had fallen back—she said, "He has done for me"—she bled very much from the lower part of her body—I raised her up, but I saw it was doing injury and I laid her down again—a doctor came and examined her and ordered her removal to the hospital, and when we arrived there in the cab she was dead—I took the prisoner into custody.
THOMAS BAKER . I am a student at Charing Cross Hospital—I live with Dr. Sutcliffe, of Battersea Park Road—on the morning of 26th August, abont 2 30, the police fetched me; I found the woman lying in Currie Street in a dying state, she was in a state of collapse—she had lost a large quantity of blood, I should think three pints—it came from the vagina—I had her put into a cab and sent to the hospital; it might have been caused by a kick.
KANIERO POKAKO . I am one of the house surgeons at St. Thomas' Hospital—on Monday night or Tuesday morning I received the deceased from the police—she was quite dead and her clothes were saturated with blood—I afterwards examined the body—the organs were healthy—there was a lacerated wound on the left side of the vagina, an inch and one-eighth in length and about an inch deep—it was such an injury as might be caused by a violent kick—she died from loss of blood arising from that injury.
The Prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Strongly recommended to mercy by the JURY on account of the provocation he received. — Three Months' Imprisonment.
[trialAccount t18790915-866]
866. TERALASCO LEONILDA (22) , Feloniously wounding James Miller, with intent to do grievous bodily harm.
LILLET Prosecuted; A. B. KELLY Defended.
JAMES MILLER . I am a labourer, and live in the Lower Road, Deptford—on 9th August, about 12 o'clock, I was in Napier Terrace near the Plough Road in company with my young woman's mother, Mary Ann Pressland; I had just parted with my young woman—I heard a scream at the top of the road, I ran up to see what was the matter and saw two men catching hold of Franklin's young woman, Miss Mayne, and pulling her into the road, they seemed to be foreigners; when I got up they ran away—my young woman came out of doors and the four of us went on to the bridge, that is my young woman and Franklin and his young woman—we were turning round to oome back home and we met three other men, foreigners—they caught hold of my young woman round the neck and pulled her off the path; I went up to the prisoner who was one of them, and pushed him away; he then struck me in the left hand and arm—one of his comrades made a run towards me and the prisoner struck him in the side—the man called out, "Oh John, no," or something like that, put his hand to his side and ran away over the bridge—I felt my hand numbed and my finger doubled up—I went back to Napier Terrace and my hand was bathed, blood was running down my arm, my coat was cut through—I was taken to the police surgeon about an hour after, and he dressed my wound—I first went to Dr. Kelly, of Plough Road, he would not look at my arm—I had given no provocation to the prisoner, only pushed him away—there was sufficient light to enable me to see him; I have no doubt about his being the man that struck me.
Cross-examined. I saw no knife in anybody's hands—I saw that my hand was covered with blood—I did not see a man named Adolphe there. I saw him afterwards at the police-court; I did not recognise him as one of the three foreigners; I could not say whether he was the man the prisoner struck, it was one of the two—he was coming towards me in a fighting attitude when the prisoner struck him.
MARY ANN PRESSLAND . I am single and live in Napier Terrace, Plough Road, Rotherhithe—on the night of 8th August, a little after 12 o'clock, I was in company with James Miller; he left me indoors and went up the street with my mother—I heard a man scream and ran up to the top of the street and saw five foreigners pulling Franklin's young woman in the road—Miller went up and the men ran over the bridge—Franklin's young woman ran down home with mother, and Franklin, me, and Miller went up on the bridge—we met three foreigners; the prisoner put his arms round my neck and Miller pushed him away and the prisoner pulled out his knife and stabbed him twice—I saw it glitter, I was close to him—then Adolphe, one of his friends, ran in between Miller, and he caught a blow in his side from the prisoner—I then returned with Miller to Napier Terrace; Franklin tame home too, but he did not stop indoors—Miller's arm was all open and the joints of the fingers of his left hand were cut and bleeding—his wounds were bathed—he had done nothing to the prisoner but push him away when he had his arm round my neck.
Cross-examined. Another foreigner was charged at the police-court with the prisoner; I don't know his name; he was discharged—the prisoner put his arm round my nock in a friendly way; my young man was
annoyed and interposed, then there was ft general confusion—I am sure it was a knife that I saw in the prisoner's hand—I did not know at the moment that Miller was stabbed, not till he was coming away.
GEORGE FRANKLIN . I am a labourer at Deptford—on 8th August I was in company with my young woman in Ploughbridge, Rotherhithe—I saw five foreigners come along, one or more of them interfered with my young woman, after a bit of a scuffle I ran over the bridge—while I was standing there I saw Miller and Pressland on the bridge with three foreigners, the prisoner was one of them—I did not see him do anything—I saw Miller after he had been home and come back again—I did not see anything the matter with his hand or arm—about half an hour afterwards I saw his father-in-law take him to the doctor's; he had his arm bound up.
THOMAS BANNISTER ( Police Inspector R). Shortly after 1 o'clock in the morning of 9th August I saw Miller at the station—he showed me his hand and arm—there was a wound on the left arm above the elbow, and one on the back of the left hand, both bleeding—I sent for the divisional surgeon, who dressed his wounds—he gave a description of the person who had wounded him, in consequence of which I went in search of the prisoner—I met him coming along with a constable—he answered the description given—he was placed with several others in the charge room, seven or eight, some of his own countrymen, and Miller picked him out at once—he war charged with cutting and wounding Miller—I afterwards went to Plough-bridge and saw a large quantity of blood on the pavement from there down to the entrance of the docks, about 300 yards—that was not in the direction in which Miller went, but the direction in which the other Italians had gone—the prisoner had no hat on when I met him—I saw the other man who was wounded at the hospital.
Cross-examined. I found a few pieces of broken pipe on the bridge—a man named Calose was charged with being concerned in this—he was subsequently discharged.
BENJAMIN BROWNING , M.R.C.S. I live at Rotherhithe—I am divisional surgeon to the police—on 9th August, between 1 and 2 in the morning, I was called to the Rotherhithe Police-station to examine Miller—he was. bleeding from two incised wounds, one on the left knuckle of the little finger, the other at the back of the same arm, below the shoulder—that was nearly four inches long; it was not very deep; it had exposed the muscle both wounds were bleeding very much—I dressed them—he was perfectly sober.
Cross-examined. I saw a knife afterwards at the police-court—such a knife as this ( produoed) would produce the wounds, but it would also produce much more severe wounds—from such a formidable weapon as this I should expect to find more severe wounds—I never saw a foreign sailor carry such a knife as this.
CHARLES ELLIOT NASH . I am a deal porter in the Surrey Commercial Docks—I found this knife and this dagger there when I was at work on the morning of 9th August—the dagger was sticking in a deal, and the sheath was lying in another place—there were marks on the dagger the colour of blood—it appeared as if it had been thrown over the fence, which is nine or—ten feet high.
THOMAS BANNISTER ( Re-examined). The fence described would be in the direction in which the men ran—a great number of foreign sailors are
employed in the ships in the docks—the prisoner was a sailor in one of those ships—I have not seen foreign sailors use a dagger similar to this; I have seen them with knives similar to this knife—I found this other knife in the prisoner's hunk on board his ship—he had not been on board since the occurrence.
GUILTY of unlawfully wounding. — Six Months' Imprisonment.

871. ALFRED SMALL (19) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. GILL, Prosecuted.
WILLIAM MORGAN . My father is a grocer, of 67, Cornhill Road, Lambeth—on 31st July, about 3 p.m., I served the prisoner with a penny bottle of ginger-beer—he gave me a florin, I gave him 1 s. 11 d. change—he drank it and went out—I put the florin in the till; there were no others there—a few minutes afterwards he came in again for a pennyworth of cake, and gave me another florin—I put it in the till and gave him 1 s. 11 d. change, and he went away—I afterwards showed the two florins to my mother and took them to the baker's shop opposite to test them, and gave them back to my mother—about a week afterwards I saw the prisoner by the coke ovens—I Had known him before, and knew where be used to live—I am sure he is the man.
MARY MORGAN . I am the wife of John Morgan—on 31st July my son brought me a florin, and I went into the shop and found another florin in the till—I found they were bad, and gave them to a policeman.
THOMAS GOODMAN ( L 85), On 5th August, about 10 o'clock, William Morgan pointed the prisoner out to me, and I took him in custody—I had received the florins before—he asked me the charge—I told him—he said "Oh, it must be a mistake; do you think I am b——silly"—I found no money on him.
WILLIAM WEBSTER . These florins are bad and from the same mould.
GUILTY .— He further PLEADED GUILTY to a conviction for felony in July, 1878.— Nine Months' Imprisonment.

872. FRANK NORTON, Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. GILL Prosecuted; MR. S. WHITE Defended.
ELEANOR HILL . I serve at the post-office, Gipsy Eill—the prisoner came
there one afternoon in July for four shillings' worth of stamps, and put dowi a florin and some other coins—I found the florin was had and passed it to the manager, who spoke to the prisoner and gave it hack to him, and he gave me good money and took the stamps away—I am sure he is the man I picked him out from a great many.
Cross-examined. This was at the end of July, hut I don't rememher the date.
FREDERICK PRICE . I manage the post-office, Lower Norwood—in July last Hill handed me a had florin and asked me in the prisoner's presence whether it was bad—I asked him where he got it—he said that he must have got it by accident—he gave me good money, and I sent somebody to follow him—I described him to Inspector Glover next day, and afterwards picked him out from 10 or 12 others.
Cross-examined. I recognised him by his personal appearance and his clothes—the other men were of different ages and wore different kinds of suite, some with whiskers and some without—I slightly bent the coin and gave it back to the prisoner.
FLORENCE HILL . I am employed at the post-office, Lower Norwood—on 11th August the prisoner came in for five shillings' worth of stamps, and gave me two florins, a sixpence, and some coppers—I gave him back one of the florins and said that it was bad—he gave me a good half-crown, I gave him sixpence change, and he left—I spoke to Mr. Corney—I saw the prisoner in custody at the station the same evening.
JONATHAN CORNET . I am assistant to Roland Ellis, of Norwood—no 11th August, a little after 4 o'clock, the prisoner came in for 5 s. worth of stamps—Hill spoke to me as he left, and I went out and spoke to Sergeant Lather, and he was taken in custody.
Cross-examined. I gave him in charge at that moment—he did not run away.
ELLEN WALLACE . I live at the Bricklayers' Arms, Lower Norwood—on 11th August the prisoner tendered a florin for a glass of ale—I took it to my mother, and went back and told him it was bad—he gave me a half-crown, I gave him the change, and he left—I picked him out on 13th August from 12 other people by his appearance.
CHARLOTTE WALLACE . My husband keeps the Bricklayers' Anns, Lower Norwood—my daughter brought me a florin on this afternoon—I bent it easily.
JAMES GLOVER ( Police Inspector P). On 11th August, about 4.15, the, prisoner was pointed out to me—I followed him about a mile—I was in uniform—I said that he was the young man I wanted—he said, "What fort"—I said, "For uttering a counterfeit two-shilling piece in the post-office in High Street"—he said, "Yes, I was there, and gave the young lady a two-shilling piece, which was bad, but I did not know it was bad"—I asked him where it was, he said that he bent it and threw it away—I asked him where he lived—he said, "In London"—I said, "It is strange you should come here to buy postage stamps"—he said that he thought they would be easier to take home than the money—I found on him a 6 d., 5 s. worth of stamps, and some bronze—he said that he was going to send the stamps home—he gave his address, 74, Shepherdess Road, Wandsworth—I went there and there was no 74.
Cross-examined. Corney pointed him out—no coins were found on him.
GUILTY — Twelve Months' Imprisonment.
[trialAccount t18790915-873]
873. GEORGE WILLIAMS (42), JOHN JONES (24), and GEORGE KITSON (38) , Burglary in the dwelling-house of Joseph George Stotten, with intent to steal.
WILLIAMS and JONES PLEADED GUILTY .
MR. GEOGHEGAN Prosecuted: MR. Purcell Defended Kitson; and MR. POYNTER Defended Jones.
MATTHEW FOX ( Police Inspector M). On Sunday night, 3rd August, I was in company with Walsh, Harvey, and Stretton, in Old Kent Road, and at 11.30—I then saw Williams and Jones—I saw them again in about half an hour, sitting on the railings in front of a house in the Old Kent Road—Harvey gave me some information at 2 o'clock, in consequence of which I went to the Duke of Kent public-house, in the Old Kent Road, with Harvey, Stretton, and Walsh—I placed a man at each door, and tried to rouse the proprietor, who leaned out of the window and spoke to me—while watching the house, the door was thrown open suddenly, and Jones and Williams attempted to rush out—I secured them, and left them in custody—I then went into the house, and found that an entrance had been effected through the fanlight—I did not see Kitson.
Cross-examined by MR. PURCELL. The last time I saw Kitson he was about 200 yards from the house—it was a fine night, no rain.
JOSEPH GEORGE STOTTEN . I am landlord of the Duke of Kent—I was aroused by the police—I cannot swear to having seen Kitson in the house before, I have seen the other two frequently.
GEORGE HARVEY ( Police Sergeant M). I was in company with Walsh, and Fox in the London Road, on the night in question—I saw the three prisoners together about 9.30—I followed them to the Duke of Kent, and about 11.15 they went in at the corner door; the house was then open—in about 10 minutes or a quarter of an hour they came out and went to another public-house, where they remained till closing time, 11—they then came out and separated—I saw them again about 12 at a coffee stall, nearly opposite the Duke of Kent—I saw them again four or five times before 2 o'clock altogether; at one time the three were standing together—I went up Trafalgar Road, and saw a light flash inside the public-house—I was standing opposite, Kitson came across, looked into my face, and passed on—I passed a word to Fox, and he placed us round the house—I took Williams—we took Kitson; he gave us an address.
JAMES WALSH ( Police Inspector). I was in company with Fox and Harvey on this night, about 9.30, in the London Road—I saw the prisoners together at 2 on the morning of the 4th outside the Duke of Kent—I saw Kitson coming from the Duke of Kent; I followed him twenty or thirty yards; he turned and looked at me; I walked on to get up to him; he walked quickly and then ran, and I after him for a considerable distance; he got into a hansom—I then turned back and assisted in taking the others—after that I was looking for Kitson,. and found him outside the Crown public-house—I took him to the station and charged him with the burglary—he said "If I knew what it was you wanted me for you should have brought me in pieces."
Cross-examined by MR. PUROELL. The Crown is a mile and a half from the Duke of Kent.
KITSON — GUILTY .
JONES and KITSON also PLEADED GUILTY to previous convictions.— Seven Years' Penal Servitude each. WILLIAMS — Eighteen Months' Imprisonment.
[trialAccount t18790915-874]
874. JAMES WALTER BROWN (40) , Feloniously forging and uttering an order for 3 l. 10 s., with intend to defraud.
MR. GEOGHEGAN Prosecuted; MR. FULTON Defended.
RICHARD COXHEAD . I am a zinc-worker, of 73, Jamaica Road—on 24th May the prisoner came and asked me to cash a cheque for 3 l. 10 s.—he said somebody at Islington had given it to him—I told him I had not the money—I took it to Mr. Evans, a neighbour, and got it cashed—Mr. Evans sent it to Mr. Johnson, and it was returned four days afterwards marked "No account.
Cross-examined. I have known the prisoner some little time, and have cashed cheques for him before, one for 25 l.—this cheque purports to be drawn by a man named Jay—the prisoner wrote his name on the back; I did not ask him to do so—I have no banker.
Re-examined. The prisoner said "The party I had it from is all right, and he has had a banking account for some years.
GEORGE ALBERT HILDT . I am manager to Mr. Wilkinson, of the Surrey Canal Saw Mills—I have received letters from the prisoner and have seen him afterwards and referred to them—I am acquainted with his handwriting—I believe the signature "William Jay" to this cheque to be his writing.
Cross-examined. I said so at the police-court when he was charged by Mr. Wilkinson with obtaining money by false pretences upon this cheque; he was admitted to bail.
Re-examined. He brought me a bill for 20 l. 10 s., which was dishonoured.
FREDERICK DEAKIN . I received this letter ( produced) from the prisoner—he told me that he had written it himself; I did not see him write it—I believe all those three documents to be in his handwriting—I gave them all to Mr. Chabot—I was at the police-court on all the occasions—no charge of forgery was definitely preferred till the last occasion, after the cheque was shown to Mr. Chabot—I had mislaid it.
Cross-examined. The prisoner was committed for trial for false pretences and admitted to bail after opposition by me—I did not know that I could prefer a bill without the permission of the Magistrate—I have no illfeeling against the prisoner.
Re-examined. I opposed his going out on bail purely in my professional capacity as a solicitor.
CHARLES CHABOT . I am an expert in handwriting—I have compared all the documents produced, and some others with this cheque—to the best of my belief the whole of the writing on the front of the cheque except the words "No account" is in the same handwriting as these documents—it is an assumed hand, not in the prisoner's natural handwriting—the endorsement "J. W. Brown" is in his natural handwriting.
Cross-examined. My evidence is based on my past experience—it has been occasionally differed from—Mr. Nethercliff had been opposed to me about seven or eight times.
CHARLES EDWIN SCARISBROOK . I am a clerk at the Holborn Branch of the Union Bank of London—this yellow cheque was issued in a book of 100 cheques to a customer of the bank, Mr. C. J. Moger, on 22nd January, 1877—the last transaction they had with us was nine or ten months ago—they have presented no cheque since—we have no customer named Jay.
RICHARD KIMBER ( Detective). I took the prisoner into custody on 30th
August—I read the warrant to him—he said, "I know nothing about it"—after he was liberated on bail I arrested him on the charge of forgery—he said, "I have been discharged for that"—I said, "No, the cheque was mislaid, and we did not go into the charge of forgery."
Cross-examined. There were two charges before the Magistrate, one for false pretences, which was dismissed, and the other was with reference to this cheque.
GUILTY .— Twelve Months' Imprisonment.
[trialAccount t18790915-875]
875. GEORGE WEBB (25) , Burglary in the dwelling-house of John Lawton Haddon, with intent to steal.
MR. WILMOT Prosecuted.
JOHN HADDON . I am a civil engineer, at 21, Lansdowne Road, Clapham—on 17th August, about 9.30,1 retired to bed, leaving some of my family up; and about 3 am. my wife woke me—I saw the outline of the prisoner standing at the foot of the bed—I caught him by the throat before he had time to move—his hands were on the mantelpiece, within a foot of my watch and ring—we have since missed a silver top of a cruet from the room he entered—I asked him how he got in; and he said, "Through the window; it was open"—I examined the premises and found foot-marks leading to the window—it looks out on to a veranda.
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. We had not found out the loss of the cruet top when before the Magistrates.
ELLEN HORSFOLD . I am servant to Mr. Haddon—I saw the windows closed about 9.45, and was the last person up.
GEORGE HAZEL ( Police Sergeant W 18). I examined the premises at 21, Lansdowe Road, and found foot-marks of two different persons in the back garden, and traced them to the balcony and found mud on the window curtains.
PETER BERRY ( Policeman W 369). I took the prisoner in custody at the door of the house, and searched him but found nothing on him.
GUILTY . He also PLEADED GUILTY to having been before convicted of felony at this Court.— Five years' Penal Servitude.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, OCTOBER 20 TH, 1879.
